# Hypothesis log

Living record of speed claims already measured. **Read a row before you measure that idea again.** After every measurement, add or update a row. Long essays stay in the linked docs; this file is the index.

A row is evidence about that day, that scene, and that config. It is not a ban. Measure again when the row is old relative to the code it touched, when the test was thin or the load did not match, or when the scene and config are not the ones in the question now. A recent row on the same scene, the same config, and a protocol-compliant pair does not need a repeat unless you want a confirm. The full rules are in `[HOW_WE_MEASURE.md](./HOW_WE_MEASURE.md)`.

Status words: `kept` · `dropped` · `screened` · `rejected-in-kernel` · `not-measured` · `superseded` · `mixed`.

**3 ms stress floor (2026-09-17):** on a stress scene, if the millisecond primary is under 3 ms on either side, the pair is **FAIL**. Three percent of 0.3 ms is timer noise. Raise that scene's own load knob. The delta is not a keep and not a drop. Gameplay (Balls, Predator, `steadyCombatScene`, zenithal, catalog `kind: 'gameplay'`) skips this floor. Kernel `timeIt` uses the same 3 ms sample floor. Constant: `STEP_MS_FLOOR` in `[tests/bench/benchmarkDefaults.mjs](../../tests/bench/benchmarkDefaults.mjs)`. Hot-path hypotheses 1–9 on this branch were measured before the floor existed. Leave those historical verdicts as they are. A new question about the same idea, on today’s code, is a new pair under the current protocol.

**8 ms campaign floor (2026-09-17):** isolation campaigns that judge `STEP_MS` need a baseline median of at least 8 ms before a speed keep, worse, or tie. If that median cannot be reached, the verdict is **FAIL / no signal**. Hygiene may stay if no primary is 3 percent worse. Kernel ops/s does not use this floor. Skip-work rows B and C were already measured at the old 2 ms floor. Those sittings stay as written. See `[HOW_WE_MEASURE.md](./HOW_WE_MEASURE.md)`.

**The shadow-caster stamp in C, W23 kernel sitting, E-114 (2026-10-23):** **screened in: +99.8 % in its kernel.** `wasm/test/shadowStamp.c` (8 658 B, a test module) is `stampLightRange` whole: the stamp grid, each light's gather, the index sort, takeClosest's quickselect and quicksort, the per-caster nearest lights with `maxShadowsPerEntity`, the append; distances are stored as float where the JS stores them, `Math.floor(..) | 0` goes through an exact ToInt32, and the lights become a table of five doubles. `shadowStampWasmMicrobench` (`bcaba94e`) on `shadowStampMicrobench`'s Predator fixture (48 lights, 16 424 casters, 512 per light, 3 per entity), every array in one shared memory, the light table written on every call: checksums equal (the stamp rows, their light indices, the cursor); alternating processes, 5 rounds (Node 22): **897.7 → 1 794.0 ops/s, +99.8 %, 25/25**; the plain path (no per-entity cap) 810 → 1 839 ops/s, 2.27×, same checksum on both sides. Kernel → scene gate: the stamp's call tree was 32 % of Predator pre-render before E-112 (`stampLightRange` 12.2, `buildStampGrid` 7.4, `partitionOrder` 6.0, `copyTypedRange` 3.7, `noteNearest` 2.8 %), about 16 % of it at 2×. Its sun rows and its output are GPU-queue regions of the render-queue memory already; the wiring adds the scratch and the light table there. Next: the wiring.

**The Predator gate after the restart: an A/A and a same-build pair (2026-10-23, night):** **noise reference: one-sided flags of 4–8 % with no code behind them.** An A/A (the same commit on both sides, 4 pairs) read REGRESSION: renderer **+6.6 % (4/4)**, physics +3.1 % (3/4), spatial +1.1 %, logic +0.1 %, pre-render −0.0 %. A same-build pair of E-112 (the emit kernel switched off on side A with `--override`, nothing else different) flagged logic +8.5 % (3/4), physics **+7.7 % (4/4)** and the renderer +4.5 % (4/4), none of which the kernel can reach, while spatial, flagged at +5.1 % (6/8) in E-112's own sitting, read +1.0 %. On this machine tonight a 4-pair sitting moves one worker or another by 4–8 %, and which one changes from sitting to sitting; an owner's sign that holds across sittings is what can be read (REVIEW_PLAN M13).

**Queue columns a multiple of 4 KB apart alias in cache, E-113 (2026-10-23, found in the W4a sitting):** **open.** The render queue's columns are `capacity × 4` bytes apart (`renderQueueLayout.js`), so at a capacity that is a multiple of 1 024 every float column of a row falls in one L1 set. In the W4a kernel, capacity 32 768 against 33 000 (same rows, same checksum): the JS emit **885 → 988 ops/s (+11 %)**, the C emit **882 → 2 150 (2.4×)**. Predator's capacity (119 298) spreads them; proceduralTree's 8 192 does not, and component columns (`count × 4` apart, element alignment only) do the same at entity totals at or next to a multiple of 1 024 (queryChurn and spawnStorm declare 8 193). Candidate: start each queue column a cache line later than the last (`renderQueueLayout` is the one layout every reader takes), and in `Component.initializeArrays` if a scene shows it (its hand-mirrored layouts in `physicsHostImpl.js` first).

**Pre-render's emit in C, E-112 (W4a, 2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `wasm/preRenderEmit.c` (3 021 B, `src/workers/preRenderEmit.wasm`) writes `emitSpriteQueue`'s full-emit rows for sprites and decorations on the render-queue memory: the sort key, the shadow terms, the tile fields and the animation clock in double as the JS does them, `proxyToGlobalAnim` and the frame tables as flat arrays. Rows of other types (particles, glows, bullets, trails) are listed and written after it by a copy of the loop's branches; play-once ends and missing textures come back as lists; Adobe and LiquidFun frames, custom layers and persist frames keep the JS loop. Wiring (`658bdb54`, `4e37b91b`, `5acea239`, `6dd088e2`): components bind at a byte offset, and SpriteRenderer, ShadowCaster and DecorationComponent live in the render-queue memory with the emit's state (lockstep visual suite: every exact scene identical); switch `wasm.preRenderEmit`. Kernel (`f46a8ae3`, Predator's mix at its queue capacity, Node 22): **976.7 → 2 124.5 ops/s, +117.5 %, 25/25**, checksums equal over 45 frames; through `emitSpriteQueue`, 30 frames with and without the kernel are byte for byte the same, other row types included. Chrome, Predator, detailed stats: `EMIT_MS` **1.24 → 0.92 ms (−26 %)**. **Predator gate against `22a9aa87`, 8 pairs, owner pre-render: 5.054 → 4.666 ms, −4.6 %, worse 2/8**; the runner read REGRESSION on spatial +5.1 % (6/8), which a same-build pair (only the kernel call switched off on side A) did not reproduce (+1.0 %) and which this session's A/A matches in size on another worker (row above); logic +0.8 %, physics +1.1 %, renderer −2.3 %, load keys within 0.1 %. Report: `reports/E-112-preRender-emit-wasm.md`.

**The stamp grid's cell computed once, E-111 (2026-10-23):** **open: ties by the rounds rule in four kernel sittings; diff in scratch.** `buildStampGrid` bins every caster twice, in its count pass and in its fill pass, each time with `Math.floor((x - minX) / 256) | 0`. Computing the cell once, by an exact multiply with 1/256 (truncation equals floor there, since x − minX ≥ 0), and keeping it in a module `Int32Array` for the fill pass: `shadowStampMicrobench` stamp case **+17.7 %** (72 % of rounds won), **+10.5 %** (73 %), **+16.7 %** (86 %), **+13.5 %** (82 %, 15 rounds), checksums equal in all four. Every sitting is a TIE under the 90 % rule; the machine was loaded each time (a demo tab and the dev server, then VS Code). Rerun on a quiet machine, or let W23's port absorb it. Diff: `D:\tmp\p4\e111.patch`.

**The per-entity stamp skips the per-light index sort, E-110 (2026-10-23):** **kept** (`5b392b7b`). The grid walk sorted each light's candidates by caster index (a typed-array sort with a JS comparator) so its rows match a serial scan. With `maxShadowsPerEntity` each kept caster only takes the light into its own slots and the rows are appended per caster afterwards, so the order inside one light changes nothing; Predator runs 3 per entity and 512 per light. `shadowStampMicrobench` (Predator fixture, 48 lights) **672 → 916 ops/s, +36.4 %**, 5/5 rounds, checksum equal. A tie-free dense test compares the grid walk with the full scan and passes on the old code too. Predator pair, pre-render owner, 4 pairs against `71008fca`: **NEUTRAL**, pre-render +0.9 % paired (2/4 worse, cv 20/12 %), with logic, physics and renderer 4–6 % faster on the same side (drift on a busy machine). The scene did not get 3 % worse, so the kernel claim stands.

**Decal stamps enqueue through a module array, E-108 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene)** (`8ad26b2a`). `Decal.stamp` wrote each resolved field into a scratch op object (a tagged field in Chrome) and passed five doubles to `enqueueStamp`: 3.2 MB/s of logic garbage in `decalStampStressScene`. Plain numbers are taken as given; x, y, scale and alpha reach the ring through a `Float64Array`, and `enqueueStamp` keeps its signature. Logic **7 923 → 4 746 KB/s**; the rest is the bench driver's config literals. A test reads back the ring for number, range and default fields, and passes on the old code too. No pair: logic there is the driver.

**Decal tile premultiply views made once, E-107 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene)** (`3c806eae`). `copyPremultiplyRgba` made two `Uint32Array` views per tile per upload: Pixi **3 658 → 76 KB/s** in `decalBlitStressScene`. Pixi keeps the views beside its tile buffers. No pair: it removes two allocations and nothing else.

**`rng()` without the closure call, E-106 (2026-10-23):** **no change; reverted.** `littleCityScene`'s logic made 3.4 MB/s in `rng` (from `cityPerson.tick`). `rng()` stepping the seeded state itself (no call to `globalThis.rng`) left it at 4.2 MB/s: the box is where `rng()` returns into a game tick too big to inline it, not inside. A double-returning API stays boxed at an uninlined caller.

**Where the rest of the garbage is (2026-10-23, heap pass over seven scenes after E-104 to E-108):** in game and bench code, mostly fractional doubles in GameObject instance fields written every tick (`painterMoveStressScene`'s `this._phase +=`: 21 MB/s; `bichosScene` 7 MB/s), which Chrome keeps as tagged fields, a heap number per write; per-entity doubles belong in a component. Engine sites left above 100 KB/s: Pixi's vendored WebGL resource bind (`for…in` over shader groups per draw, 500–700 KB/s in Predator, bichos, littleCity), `_applyContactBegin`'s pair map (75–170 KB/s, W18), `_emscripten_get_now` in the Box2D thread (210–340 KB/s), Pixi `updateLighting` (220–270 KB/s in Predator), `syncParticleFeed` (80–170 KB/s).

**The persist check and the pose pass in one walk, E-98h (2026-10-23):** **dropped (tie); diff in scratch.** After E-98g BunnyMark's pre-render profile put the persist check at 23 % and the poses-only pass at 17 %, two walks of every row; one walk (`_type0PersistPoses`, −1 on a miss) did both, with the animated rows advanced only after every row passed (a walk that misses on a late row would otherwise advance them twice, once there and once in the full emit; a new test fails a mutant that advances inline). 4 pairs, `bunnyMarkScene`, pre-render owner against `74f41130`: pre-render **6.174 → 6.244 ms, −0.3 % paired, worse 2/4**, renderer +0.4 %. The two walks' shared loads were not the cost; each row's own work is. Reverted; the diff is `E-98h.fused-persist.diff` in the session scratch.

**Query type masks as index lists, E-104 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene)** (`564fccc4`). `copyActiveMatchesToBuffer`, `query()` and `_buildQueryResult` walked each type mask in BigInt (`countTrailingZeros`, `mask &= mask - 1n`), which allocates per step; publishing the precomputed queries every frame made 199 KB/s in each Predator logic worker. Each mask's type indices are an `Int32Array` built with the query, lowest first (the bit walk's order); the class version's closure per query per publish went too. With E-105, one Predator pair (below). A test walks a sparse 64-type mask (types 0, 5, 33, 63) through `query()`, the slow active path and the publish, and passes on the old code as well.

**FSM time in whole milliseconds, E-105 (2026-10-23):** **kept** (`6b2ed599`). `FSM.tick` passed `FSMClass.time[i]` (a fractional Float32 read) to the state's `onUpdate`, a call TurboFan does not inline (one `onUpdate` per state class): a heap number per tick. The sampling profiler charged it to the game tick that inlined `FSM.tick` (`civilian.js:76` 1 663 KB/s, `person.js:179` 837, `mySoldier.js:60` 347, `fsm.js:136` 340–350). The time now crosses as `time | 0`, whole ms; the column keeps its fractions, and the doc and the type say whole ms. Predator logic garbage with E-104: **2 848 / 2 672 → 212 / 272 KB/s per worker, −92 %** (heapSampleWorkers, 6 s each). Predator, logic owner against `74f41130` (before both), 4 pairs: logic **4.458 → 4.307 ms, −1.9 % paired, worse 2/4** (cv 12/2 %), no worker 3 % worse (spatial +2.6 %, pre-render +1.6 %), load keys within 0.1 %: NEUTRAL, which tonight's A/A says a four-pair Predator verdict cannot resolve below 5 %. E-68 (2026-10-21) screened this as a public signature change for ~0.5 %, from 0.8 MB/s: the signature stays, the value is whole ms, and the sites inlined into game ticks had not been counted.

**The spatial row rebuild in C, W6 screen (2026-10-23):** **screened (ceiling): +10.9 % in its kernel, about 1.4 % of Predator spatial.** `wasm/test/spatialRebuild.c` is `rebuildOwnedRows` with `getColliderBounds` and `getCellRange` inlined, every shape path, the JS arithmetic in double. `spatialRebuildWasmMicrobench` moves every column the rebuild reads into the spatial publish memory (as Box2D's memory would hold them, Phase 4), so both sides read the same bytes; same checksum over 45 frames of cells, poses and owned lists on Predator's mix and on a mix of every shape. `runKernelVariants`, 5 alternating rounds, Predator's mix (16 500 circles, one worker): JS **2 715 → 3 011 ops/s, +10.9 %**. On the every-shape mix the C is 1.48× (0.85 → 0.58 ms): the JS pays for the non-circle bounds call, not for the loop. The rebuild is 12.5 % of Predator spatial and its JS is close to the floor of its scattered cell writes, so W6 is worth about 1.4 % there; W7 is left with the glue (8 %), and spatial stops being the first reason for the Phase 4 move. Spatial's garbage in Predator, sampled the same day: 17.8 KB/s, nothing to take.

**Predator gate, E-98e to E-98g and the isolation commits (2026-10-23):** **passed on the A/A (NEUTRAL).** `runPredatorAbab --name gate-E98e-g --vs a5292000 --owner preRender`. Four pairs said REGRESSION with every worker worse (pre-render +7.1 %, physics +14.3 %, spatial +4.0 %, renderer +3.3 %, cv 10–19 % on the baseline) while two read-only helper agents ran on the machine; four more pairs on an idle machine (`--append`): pre-render **5.662 → 5.766 ms, −0.3 % paired, worse 4/8**, spatial +6.8 % (6/8), physics +2.9 % (6/8), renderer +1.8 % (5/8), logic +0.9 %, load keys within 0.2 %. Spatial is a worker no commit in the batch reaches. An A/A against the same commit right after (`AA-predator-1023b`, `--vs d3060a97`, 4 pairs) moved every worker one way: pre-render **−5.0 %, worse 0/4** (a "keep" by the rule), logic −7.4 %, renderer −3.9 %, spatial −3.2 % (0/4), physics −2.7 %. On this machine tonight a four-pair Predator verdict cannot see 5 %, and spatial's +6.8 % is inside that: no regression. The kept changes stand on their own scene pairs.

**A frame the other buffer moved is repaired, not a full emit, E-98g (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** The queue's two buffers alternate. When one buffer's frame advanced an animated sprite, the other still remembered the old frame, and its persist check missed: in a scene of animated sprites nearly every frame became a full emit (a unit test with two sprites on different periods: 20 persist frames of 60). The check now gives that row the current frame's texture (`_repairPersistFrame`, the full emit's `animStart + frame`; it declines, and the frame misses, when there is no animation entry or the frame is past the animation's end), after the animation-state test so a changed animation still misses; `_advancePersistFrame` keeps the buffer's remembered frame in step; a repair bumps `staticGen`. The test: 58 of 60, each buffer showing the current frame on every tick. `animatedSpriteStressScene` (new, `7ef92576`: 100 000 civilians walking on the spot at different speeds, all in view), 4 pairs, pre-render owner against `7ef92576`: pre-render **8.887 → 7.098 ms, −19.3 %, worse 0/4**, cv 3/5 %; renderer below its floor; presented 60 both sides; `VISIBLE_ENTITIES` 100 000 both. For the next Predator gate with E-98e/f.

**Bodiless sprites read their pose in the collect, E-98f (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** After E-98e the collect still called `_displayPose` per sprite (14 % self of BunnyMark's pre-render), whose Transform branch is all a sprite without a body needs. Both collect copies read that branch inline (the Transform columns the loop already holds, rotation as `_displayPose` reads it); a sprite whose body has a published pose still calls `_displayPose`. The parity test gained bodies (one with a dead pose rotation, which `_displayPose` sends to the Transform) and Transform rotations; two mutants fail it. 4 pairs, `bunnyMarkScene`, pre-render owner against `25d0c3da`: pre-render **7.441 → 6.070 ms, −18.4 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/2 %; renderer +0.7 % (4/4, under 3 %); presented 60 both sides; `VISIBLE_ENTITIES` 250 000 both. For the next Predator gate with E-98e.

**The sprite collect writes its own rows, E-98e (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** After E-98d, BunnyMark's pre-render spent 39 % of its busy time in `_writeRenderable`, called once per sprite by E-98c's shortcut: each call re-read the collector's columns, the pose scratch, the custom-collector table and the counters from `this`. The shortcut's sprites always go to the main collector, so both copies of the collect loop (turned camera and not) now write those rows themselves with the columns taken once before the loop; the count lives in a local, synced around `collectRenderable` (masked sprites) and after the loop. A behavioural test fills the collectors through the loop and through `collectRenderable` for every sprite and compares every column, Y-sort on and off (two mutants of the shortcut fail it). 4 pairs, `bunnyMarkScene`, pre-render owner against `a5292000`: pre-render **8.690 → 7.314 ms, −15.8 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/1 %; renderer −0.4 %; presented 60 both sides; `VISIBLE_ENTITIES` 250 000 both. Touches every scene's sprite collect: for the next Predator gate.

**The transmit wedge without heap numbers, E-96c (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene).** After E-96b, `_buildSelfLitBatch` allocated 6.5 MB/s at `?selfLit=65536`: `_emitTransmitWedge` took nine doubles per occluder per light and does not inline into the batch, so each call boxed them, although with `block = 1` (every committed scene) it returns at its first line. The batch now calls it only for `block < 1`. On that path the light and the occluder's pose go through a module `Float64Array` (`WEDGE_IN`) and only the entity crosses the call; `_pushWedge`, the tangent helpers and copies of `writeOrientedBoxVerts` / `writePolygonVerts` (same arithmetic, term for term) read them there. Chrome heap pass, Pixi: **6.8 → 0.32 MB/s** at `block = 1`; with every occluder at `block = 0.5` (`?block=X`, `dad840c4`, the first committed scene with transmit wedges) 8.4 → 3.5 MB/s after the arguments alone, **0.33 MB/s** with the helpers. Screenshots with box and circle wedges: 0 of 921 600 pixels differ. No timing pair: Pixi stays under the 3 ms floor at every load the scene reaches (2.2 ms with 2 100 occluders, where pre-render reaches 16 ms), so a pair would FAIL without deciding; the change only removes work.

**Every light's self-lit fills in one draw, E-96b (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** After E-96 the self-lit pass (each occluder's own footprint lit again, with a transmit wedge behind occluders that let light through) still made a render call per light: 46 % of Pixi's busy time, inclusive, mostly Pixi's setup per call. Every light's fills, then its wedges (the order the two per-light meshes drew in), now go into one mesh with the light's terms as vertex attributes (52-byte vertices; a wedge's facing radius `1e30` makes its facing term 1), drawn in the light pass after the fans; sprite-masked occluders still draw a pass per light. Screenshots at the same moment: 0 of 921 600 pixels differ on WebGL and WebGPU, with circles, and with a scratch variant at `block = 0.5` (wedges); the lockstep suite's new `vispoly` and `compoundvis` scenes identical. The engine's `maxOccluderSelfLit` of 512 gives only a few dozen lights a pass, so the scene got `?selfLit=N` (`b51de446`). 4 pairs, `visPolyStressScene?selfLit=65536`, renderer owner against `7fb780bf`: renderer **3.395 → 1.197 ms, −64.9 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/1 %; pre-render −1.0 %; presented 60 both sides. Pixi allocation at the default cap **8.7 → 1.8 MB/s**. Report: [`reports/E-96b-self-lit-batch.md`](./reports/E-96b-self-lit-batch.md).

**Predator gate, E-87 to E-103 (2026-10-23):** **passed (NEUTRAL).** One gate for the batch on shared paths (the body dirty drain by halves, the slim ray entry points, the visibility copy clamp, the aliased pose columns, the batched light mesh, the tile-column skip and the painter run sort): `runPredatorAbab --name gate-E87-E103 --vs 89aa64d7 --owner preRender`, 4 pairs. Pre-render **5.117 → 4.999 ms, −1.3 %, worse 1/4**, cv 2/4 %; logic +2.7 % (3/4, cv 11/4 %, under 3 %), spatial −0.8 %, physics −3.7 %, renderer −1.6 %; presented frames 60 on both sides; load keys within 0.1 %. Predator draws no Adobe Animate character and no visibility polygon, so E-96, E-102b and E-103 have nothing to win there; the gate shows they cost it nothing.

**`TileMap.getTileId`, E-83 (2026-10-23):** **answered, no change.** `tilemapStressScene` spends 566 ms/s of logic, 80.3 % self in `getTileId` (Q1b), because its 64 queriers ask 16 500 tiles each per tick: 63 M queries/s. `tileMapMicrobench` (Node, the same call with a layer name) reads **2.5 ns per query**. The scene's share (≈7 ns per query under the CPU profiler, Chrome) is the query count, with the profiler's overhead on a call that small; a game asks thousands of tiles per frame, not millions. No sitting: nothing it could find would change a game's frame.

**The painter sorts runs of equal keys as one, E-103 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** An Adobe Animate character writes its pieces into consecutive queue slots under one sort key, and the full painter radix sorted every piece (`radixSortIndicesBySortKey` 17.5 % + `radixPainterOrder` 4.5 % of the saturated pre-render in `adobeAnimateScene`). `radixPainterOrder` now finds maximal runs (consecutive slots, the same key bits), sorts the runs' first slots with the same stable radix and expands each run in place: a stable sort of every row keeps a run together at its first row's place among equal keys, so the order is identical (`painterRunSort.test.js` against the plain radix: dense, filtered with gaps, ties across runs, no runs). When runs do not halve the rows (checked after 1 024 rows) it sorts the rows as before. `painterRadixMicrobench` against HEAD: new `pieces` case **+287 %**, catalog `mixed` +4.1 % (96 % of rounds), `sprites` +1.7 %. 4 pairs, pre-render owner, `--camera-zoom min`, against `186870f6`: pre-render **23.452 → 21.157 ms, −9.8 %, worse 0/4**, cv 0/2 %; renderer −9.8 % (1/4, cv 12 %).

**Tile columns cleared only once something tiled, E-102b (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Every Adobe piece row, every non-sprite row and every plain sprite row wrote zeros into the queue's repeat and tile columns (`clearTileFields`, 8.2 % self of the saturated pre-render in `adobeAnimateScene`). A queue's columns start at zero and only the repeating-sprite branch writes anything else, so the emit keeps the set of tileMode columns that branch has written (per queue buffer and custom layer) and skips the zero writes until its column is in it. Lockstep balls and oriented pixel-exact; a test reuses a repeating sprite's row for a plain one. 4 pairs, pre-render owner, `--camera-zoom min`, against `3c8c7736`: pre-render **26.066 → 23.613 ms, −10.2 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/2 %; renderer +5.4 % at 2/4, cv 25 % (noise). Touches Predator's full emit: for the next Predator gate.

**The Adobe piece loop with its columns hoisted, E-102 (2026-10-23):** **rejected (regression), reverted.** `_emitAdobePieces` (36 % of a saturated pre-render in `adobeAnimateScene` at the minimum zoom) read every queue column through `ref` per piece, beside two calls TurboFan left out (`_setQueueType`, `clearTileFields`, the latter 8.2 % self). The 23 columns went into locals before the loop and the two helpers inline; same stores, same values. 4 pairs, pre-render owner, `--camera-zoom min`, against `3c8c7736`: pre-render **26.478 → 27.194 ms, +3.6 %, worse 4/4**, cv 2/2 %. Twenty-three more live values in a function this size most likely spilled registers inside the loop; the reloads were cheaper. Same lesson as review R8: measure a hot-loop restructuring, do not assume it.

**The kernels' build-flag pass (2026-10-23):** measured, no change. Every shipped kernel (`spatialPublish`, `spatialGather`, `particleIntegrate`, `particleLists`, `gpuPack`) rebuilt with `-mnontrapping-fptoint`: **byte-identical** binaries, because the pinned clang (24.0.0git) already enables it for wasm32. With wasm-opt `-O4` instead of `-O3`: `gpuPack` identical; the other four differ and tie in their own microbenches (Node 22, 5 alternating rounds, one process per run): `spatialGather` +0.5 %, `particleIntegrate` +1.4 % (4/5), `particleLists` −0.6 to +0.7 % across its four cases, `spatialPublish` −0.5 %. `-O3` stays. Not run in Chrome: `-O4` only reorders Binaryen's passes and TurboFan reoptimizes the module; a kernel's own scene sitting is the place to see a difference if one exists. SIMD and float32 per kernel stand as recorded (W8a chose scalar double for correctness, E-18 rejected SIMD for the gather pack).

**Pixi's sprite pack through gpuPack.wasm, W9d (2026-10-23):** **rejected (logic regression in Predator); code kept outside the tree.** With `packGpuSprites: 'pixi'` and no pose interpolation (Predator), Pixi's entity batch took its rows from new regions of the render-queue memory, and its plain upload went through the W9 kernel on Pixi's own scratch, falling back to JS where the kernel declines; bit-identical against a JS-packed batch, and every Predator pack took the kernel (301 of 301 counted). Predator, renderer owner against `3e2dc99c`, 8 pairs: renderer **3.563 → 3.317 ms, −9.1 %, worse 0/8**, but logic **3.409 → 3.755 ms, +5.9 %, worse 7/8** (cv 7/7 %), which the change cannot reach. Controls the same day: an A/A of Predator (4 pairs: logic −0.9 %, physics +3.5 % at 3/4, so 4-pair flags of that size happen by chance), and W9d's memory layout with the kernel switched off (8 pairs: logic +2.7 %, worse 5/8, NEUTRAL). So logic paid when Pixi got faster (+0.35 ms against Pixi's −0.25 ms), not for the layout; the likely path is a faster Pixi letting pre-render's collect overlap logic's tick on shared cache. Diff and test in scratch, for when the workers' phasing changes.

**A/A of Predator, the session's noise reference (2026-10-23):** `runPredatorAbab --vs HEAD` with the tree at HEAD, 4 pairs: logic −0.9 %, spatial −0.6 %, physics **+3.5 % (worse 3/4, cv 9/2 %, flagged REGRESSION)**, renderer +1.9 %, pre-render +0.3 %. A single 4-pair Predator flag near 3 % is not evidence by itself.

**Every light's disc and fan in one draw, E-96 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Raycasted lighting drew two meshes per light (umbra disc, visibility fan), each with its own uniforms: over 700 draws a frame in `visPolyStressScene`, Pixi's per-draw binding and buffer bookkeeping most of its step. The light's terms are now vertex attributes (40-byte interleaved vertices), every light's disc then fan in light order in one mesh, 32-bit indices, buffers grown to a high-water mark; new GLSL ES 3.00 and WGSL shaders read them as flat varyings. Screenshots at the same moment: 0 of 921 600 pixels differ on WebGL and on WebGPU. 4 pairs, renderer owner against `3995c846`: renderer **5.472 → 1.361 ms, −75.2 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/3 %; presented 60 both sides; Pixi allocation **36.5 → 8.7 MB/s**. Report: [`reports/E-96-light-batch.md`](./reports/E-96-light-batch.md).

**Decal stamping in WASM, W26 screen (2026-10-23):** **scalar port ties JS; parked.** A freestanding C port of `Decal.stampToTileBuffers` for a batch of stamps (64-byte records, tiles at byte 0 of a shared memory, textures beside them, touched tiles listed for the caller to flag; JS-exact ToInt32 and clamped stores) wrote the same bytes as the JS path: 0 of 2 621 440 differing, the same 40 tiles touched (5 000 seeded stamps, dense and sparse textures, both blend modes, 8×5 tiles of 128 px). Node, 9 rounds: JS 49.9 ms, WASM 50.2 ms (×0.99). Plain casts instead of ToInt32 in the pixel loop: 50.3 ms. The loop is bound by its byte traffic and per-pixel branches, which TurboFan compiles as well as clang. Left: SIMD blending with a ±1 tolerance (speed over exactness), or stamping on the GPU into tile render textures (no pixel loop, no upload; changes how saves read tiles). Source kept outside the tree.

**The decal pixel loop split by blend mode, E-100 (2026-10-23):** **rejected in the kernel.** `stampToTileBuffers` tested `blendMode === 1` per pixel; two copies of the tile's pixel loop, one per mode, matched the reference (400 stamps, same checksum). `decalMicrobench`, `runKernelsVsRev --vs HEAD`, 5 rounds: primary `normal_multitile_large` 6 731 → 6 710 ops/s (−0.3 %, a tie); `normal_1tile_dense` 0.0 %, `normal_sparse` 0.0 %, `multiply_sparse` −1.9 %, `multiply_1tile_dense` −4.8 %. TurboFan already handled the branch; reverted. The JS loop is near what JS does here: W26 (WASM, SIMD blend) is the remaining lever.

**Caster positions in bin order for the shadow stamp, E-99 (2026-10-23):** **rejected in the kernel.** Predator's CPU profile put 42 % of `stampLightRange` on reading `sun[c * floats]` for each binned caster, so `buildStampGrid` also copied x and y into arrays in bin order and the light walk read those in sequence (bit-identical; `gpuShadowCasters.test.js` passed). `shadowStampMicrobench` (Predator fixture, 48 lights, 16 424 casters), `runKernelsVsRev --vs HEAD`, 5 rounds: stamp **666 → 658 ops/s, −1.2 %**, rounds won 8 %: a tie. The 1.4 MB of caster rows stay in cache; the line's samples were load stalls, not a gather order could fix. Reverted. Busy time in the CPU trace also misled elsewhere this day: Predator's three Box2D pool threads read 1000 ms/s while all of Chrome used 1.9 cores (OS process time), because a thread blocked in `Atomics.wait` samples as busy.

**Poses collected straight into the queue, E-98d (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** The collect stashed each pose (x, y, rotation) and the persist frame copied the stash into the queue's columns. An Adobe root is the only row the emit expands into several, so on a frame without one queue row i is collector row i: the collect writes the poses into the main queue's own columns, and the persist pass (a copied loop) only writes the rotation of sprites drawn with their own facing. With an Adobe root the stash is used as before. Lockstep balls and oriented pixel-exact; the persist test runs both arrangements. `bunnyMarkScene`, 4 pairs, pre-render owner against `63b7dd38`: pre-render **10.100 → 8.665 ms, −14.1 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/0 %. The E-98 series: 21.1 → 8.7 ms per new queue.

**The visibility polygon copy without views, N-1 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene).** `buildVisibilityPolygons` copied each light's polygon with `f32.set(outX.subarray(0, n), …)`, two views per light per frame (2.2 MB/s in `visPolyStressScene`, the E-95 heap pass); a loop copies the same Float32 values, clamped to the output length as `subarray` was. Screenshot unchanged. `visPolyStressScene`, 4 pairs, pre-render owner against `f052f897`: pre-render 5.222 → 5.151 ms (−0.3 %, worse 1/4), renderer −0.6 %.

**Public `Ray` methods that inline into game code, E-90 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene).** Game code calling `Ray.cast`, `castAll`, `castDir`, `castWithInfo`, `linecast` or `linecastDir` with doubles boxed each double: the methods (stats `try`/`finally`, setup math) were too big for TurboFan to inline. Each now writes its doubles into `RAY` and calls a body that takes integers and objects, with the stats wrapper in a separate `_xTimed` taken only when `collectDetailedStats` is on. Shrinking those halved the driver's allocation (17.3 → 8.7 MB/s); `hasLineOfSight` and `linecastBetweenEntities`, called in the same function, kept their `try`/`finally`, and inlining budget is per caller: slimmed the same way, the logic worker went **17.6 → 0.29 MB/s** (`rayStressScene?casts=12288`, Chrome heap pass). Pair, 4 pairs, logic owner against `f7d750a6`: logic **9.298 → 9.112 ms, −1.9 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/0 %: a tie on time.

**The body dirty words in 16 bits, E-87 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `markBodyDirty`'s `Atomics.or` on a 32-bit word with bit 30 or 31 set returned a heap number in Chrome (Atomics run as builtins there): 3.66 MB/s in `spawnStormScene`'s logic worker, and physics' drain `Atomics.exchange` 153 KB/s. Both sides now use a `Uint16Array` over the same bytes (little-endian, so entity i is the same bit as before and the scene's all-dirty fill is unchanged); the drain reads each 32-bit word plainly first and exchanges only the 16-bit halves of a non-empty one. Heap pass after: both 0. `tests/node/bodyDirtyDrain.test.js` runs physics' real drain over words the real marker wrote. `spawnStormScene`, 4 pairs, logic owner against `89aa64d7`: logic **5.302 → 5.184 ms, −4.0 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/2 %; physics 2.115 → 2.063 ms (below its floor); load keys within 5 %.

**Predator gate, E-97 to E-98c (2026-10-23):** **passed (NEUTRAL).** One gate for the batch on shared paths (frame pacing on WebGL and WebGPU, the split pose upload, N-64 and E-98a–c in pre-render's collect and emit): `runPredatorAbab --vs 0cd085bd --owner renderer`, 4 pairs. Pre-render **5.537 → 5.071 ms, −8.9 %, worse 0/4**; renderer −0.5 %, logic −6.3 %, physics −1.3 %, spatial +0.7 % (2/4); presented frames 60 on both sides; load keys within 1 %.

**Unmasked sprites straight to the entities collector, E-98c (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** The sprite collect loops called `collectRenderable(0, i)` for every visible sprite: a type dispatch, `Layer.entitiesMask()` and a bit scan that, for a sprite with no layer mask, always end at `_writeRenderable(0, i, Layer.entitiesId)`. The loops now call that directly for unmasked sprites; masked ones keep the generic path (`spriteCollectShortcut.test.js` checks both and that the generic path picks the same collector). `bunnyMarkScene`, 4 pairs against `f8a0ebb6`: pre-render **11.753 → 10.053 ms, −14.2 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/1 %, more than `collectRenderable`'s 13 % self share (likely `_writeRenderable` now inlines with a constant type, not checked). Since E-97: BunnyMark pre-render 21.1 → 10.1 ms per new queue.

**No emit loop on a sprites-only persist frame, E-98b (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** On a persist frame whose rows are all sprites, the emit loop only counted the rows (and computed a sort key per row it then skipped); the count is set directly, and the key is computed after the persist skip. Frame moves of animated sprites go to their own count for `staticGen`. `bunnyMarkScene`, 4 pairs, pre-render owner against `1d32263d`: pre-render **12.319 → 11.833 ms, −3.7 %, worse 1/4**, cv 0/3 %; renderer and presented frames unchanged.

**Animations advance on persist frames, N-64 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene).** A bug fix: the persist path skipped the only place sprite animations advanced, so a scene whose rows held still froze them (REVIEW_PLAN N-64). The persist loop reads `isAnimated` per sprite row and runs the clock for animated ones. `bunnyMarkScene` (none animated), 4 pairs against `973f15b9`: pre-render **12.027 → 12.216 ms, +0.9 %** (4/4, cv 1/1 %), renderer +0.4 %, presented 60 both sides.

**No sprite sort line without Y-sort, E-98a (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Pre-render computed every collected sprite's sort line (foot Y, a frame lookup when not feet-anchored, the rounded key) although without Y-sort the key is the zIndex alone (`orderSortKey` ignores the Y). It is computed now only where the destination layer Y-sorts; an Adobe root's key still is (its pieces write it). Lockstep visual, balls and oriented: pixels exact. `bunnyMarkScene`, headed, 4 pairs, pre-render owner against `f044d907`: pre-render **18.943 → 12.014 ms, −36.4 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/1 %; renderer −7.2 %; presented frames 60 both sides.

**Pixi paces WebGPU frames too, E-97b (2026-10-23):** **kept (product); the gate's renderer flag overruled with a CPU trace.** The pacer counts `queue.onSubmittedWorkDone()` completions against submissions (they resolve in order) and waits while the frame before last is unfinished. `bunnyMarkScene?backend=webgpu` (`e55c15a0`), headed, 4 pairs, `--owner presented`: presented frames **43.4 → 60.0 fps, +38.4 %, 4/4 higher**, cv 2/0 %. The gate said REGRESSION on renderer `STEP_MS` 3.311 → 4.277 ms (+29.2 %, 4/4, cv 4/2 %). A CPU trace of each side: Pixi busy 131 ms/s at 41 fps against 175 ms/s at 60, **3.2 → 2.9 ms per presented frame**; pre-render 572 → 794 and logic 98 → 141 ms/s, all running more frames. Unpaced, Pixi's mean mixed upload frames with cheap redraws the compositor dropped. HOW_WE_MEASURE now says how to read this.

**The split instance upload on top of E-97, E-82 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** The stashed split, unchanged, against `f59fa284` (E-97 on both sides). `bunnyMarkScene`, headed, 4 pairs, renderer owner: renderer **13.122 → 4.265 ms, −67.7 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/2 %; presented frames 59.9 → 60.0; pre-render 21.110 → 19.084 ms (−9.1 %, worse 0/4: likely less memory traffic beside it, not separated). The split also renders on WebGPU (checked on screen). WebGPU BunnyMark presents **43.2 fps** with Pixi at 2.5 ms: the E-97 symptom, unpaced there (E-97b). Report: [`reports/E-82-split-instance-upload.md`](./reports/E-82-split-instance-upload.md).

**Pixi paces its frames to the GPU, E-97 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** A WebGL2 fence after each frame, read two frames later; while it is unsignaled Pixi's tick submits nothing and takes no queue (lockstep always draws, a lost context never waits, eight ticks at most). `bunnyMarkScene` (250 000 bunnies, GPU 3D engine at ~98 % on both sides), headed, 4 pairs, `--owner presented` against `0cd085bd`: presented frames **54.8 → 59.9 fps, +9.1 %, 4/4 higher**, cv 1/0 %. Renderer `STEP_MS` 10.125 → 13.161 ms (+31 %, cv 36 %, not blocking) is the frame mix: HEAD's mean included cheap redraws the compositor dropped, and paced Pixi draws 60 full frames at ~13 ms, about the 803 ms/s HEAD's CPU trace showed. Pre-render 21.682 → 20.970 ms, per worked frame. WebGPU is not paced (E-97b). Report: [`reports/E-97-gpu-frame-pacing.md`](./reports/E-97-gpu-frame-pacing.md).

**The split instance upload, E-82 first sitting (2026-10-23):** **regression on presented frames; waits on E-97.** The pose stream (x, y, rotation, previous x, y: 24 bytes) every frame and the statics (44 bytes) per ring slot only when pre-render's new `staticGen` column moves. `bunnyMarkScene`, 4 pairs, renderer owner against `0937669a`: renderer **13.603 → 3.238 ms, −76.4 %, worse 0/4**, but the page **55 → 44 fps** in every pair and pre-render +217 % (cv 30 %). Pre-render's busy time was the same on both sides (CPU trace 603 vs 611 ms/s): its mean had mixed in near-zero backpressure skips, fixed in `0cd085bd`. The frames: with the statics forced up every frame, the split layout presented 54.6 fps, and with a fence check in Pixi 60.0. A fast Pixi oversubscribed a full GPU (E-97). The ceiling screen's pre-render and main-thread moves below were this, not its hack. The gate now judges presented frames (`40bd881d`). Next: the split again on top of E-97.

**Instance upload ceiling, E-82 screen (2026-10-23):** **the split is worth building.** A temporary hack in `_finishUpload` uploaded 24 of the 68 bytes per pose-interpolated instance (the packing still wrote all 68). `bunnyMarkScene`, 250 000 bunnies, headed Chrome, 2 pairs: renderer **14.040 → 7.595 ms, −44.5 %**, worse 0/2. Pre-render (+118 %) and the main thread (56 → 41 fps) moved with it because the GPU drew the garbage instances (huge stale sprites, overdraw): artifacts of the hack, not costs of a split. About half of Pixi's step at this size is the transfer itself. Next: the per-frame stream (x, y, previous x, y, rotation: 24 bytes) apart from the statics, which are uploaded when pre-render reports a full rewrite.

**The visibility sweep without heap numbers, E-95 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `visPolyStressScene`'s pre-render allocated 37 MB/s in the per-light sweep (`buildVisibilityPolygon` 19.8, `emitVertex` 12.0, `distSqToConvex` 2.6 MB/s). Four causes, found by bisecting a copy of the module under `heapSampleNode.mjs`: (1) doubles passed between functions too big to inline (the sample direction into `emitVertex`, an arc's angles into `emitArcVertices`) and returned (`rayCircleDist`, `rayPolyDist`, `distSqToConvex`): now in a module `Float64Array`, the light still passed through; (2) `let minDist = maxRadius` in `emitVertex`: the parameter had not been used as a number yet, so the loop's merge was typed any value and every closer hit boxed (`+maxRadius` fixes it); (3) `normalizeAngleSigned` at four call sites in a function so big that the inlining budget took one: the angles are normalised in place in two slots by a copy of its arithmetic; (4) `tangentHalfAngle` then fell out of the budget: inlined by hand. Bit-identical: 2 000 random light and occluder sets, 92 282 vertices, every one equal to the old code's. Chrome heap pass, pre-render **37.0 → 2.7 MB/s** (left: `subarray` per light in `buildVisibilityPolygons`, 2.2 MB/s). Pair (4 pairs): pre-render **5.538 → 5.280 ms, −4.1 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/2 %. W2a (the sweep in WASM) now starts from this JS.

**Predator gate, W9 + W9b (2026-10-23):** **passed on the second sitting.** `runPredatorAbab --vs 5f170e34 --owner preRender`, 4 pairs: the first flagged **physics +3.6 %, worse 3/4** (cv 5/2 %), a worker the pack cannot reach, with pre-render −1.7 % and the rest within 1 %. The rule, set before the second sitting: physics ≥ +3 % at 3 of 4 worse again and the scatter at Predator's size is suspect. The second: physics **−7.9 %** (2/4), pre-render −2.1 %, spatial −4.6 %, renderer −5.9 %, NEUTRAL. Predator's physics moved −9.3 % to +6.7 % across today's gates with no change reaching it: the first flag was drift.

**Pixi's pack as a scatter, E-94 W9c (2026-10-23):** **rejected (regression), reverted.** The W9b order in Pixi's JS pack (`packInstancedRows`' sprite path; Predator packs its sprites in Pixi): an inverse of the painter list, the queue read in order, rows written to their slots, for lists of ≥ 4 096 rows over half the queue. Bit-identical (a test against the kernel's gather). Predator pair (4 pairs, renderer owner): renderer **3.491 → 3.769 ms, +6.4 %, worse 3/4**, cv 2/6 %. At 13 400 rows the queue (650 KB) stays in cache, so the inverse's two extra passes and the scattered writes cost more than the gather's misses; W9b won on a 14 MB queue. The lockstep run also caught a crash on the way (Pixi builds its pack context in `_beginUpload`, without the field the scatter used). A threshold of tens of thousands of rows might still pay in Pixi; not tried.

**The pack as a scatter, E-94 W9b (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** At 300 000 rows the WASM pack was bound by its painter-order gather (18 columns read in an order that jumps across a 14 MB queue). `pack_sprites_scatter` / `pack_casters_scatter` build the inverse of the index list, then read the queue in order and write each listed row to its slot; a repeated or out-of-range row makes the kernel decline (-1) and the gather packs. The client scatters lists of at least 4 096 rows that cover half the queue. Bit-identical (`gpuPackScatter.test.js`, which also checks that the scatter ran). An isolated Chrome worker showed only 1.17× over the gather at 300 000 rows on a warm queue; in the scene the queue is cold. **`painterSortStressScene` pair (4 pairs): pre-render 42.474 → 34.324 ms, −18.3 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/2 %; with W9, 44.446 → 34.3 ms. Renderer 2.64 → 2.92 ms, under the floor. Next: the same order in Pixi's pack (Predator packs there).

**Instanced-row pack in WASM, E-94 / E-76 W9 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `wasm/gpuPack.c` packs sprite rows (15 floats) and caster rows (22) as `packInstancedRows` does on its fast paths (world space, no snap, no type filter), with JS's arithmetic, bit-identical (`gpuPackWasm.test.js`). The render queues and GPU queues live in one shared `WebAssembly.Memory` (`f7ad9411`) so the kernel reads the rows where pre-render wrote them; pre-render's `_packRows` takes the kernel and falls back to JS for anything else (Pixi-side packs, the cookie queue, screen space). Kernel (16k Predator rows, both variants on the memory): sprites **3.1×**, casters **3.1×** (Node); a Chrome worker: 2.33× and 2.9–3.0×. Lockstep visual suite: every pixel-compared scene identical. `painterSortStressScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off, 300 000 rows): pre-render **44.446 → 42.561 ms, −3.5 %, worse 0/4**, cv 1/1 %. Smaller than the pack's 38.9 % share predicts: the CPU profile puts `pack_sprites` at 33.6 % (3 753 ms against `packInstancedRows`' 4 404 in the same window), so at 300 000 rows the pack is bound by its gather in painter order across a 14 MB queue, not by arithmetic. Next: a scatter form (rows read in queue order, written to their painter slot), and the Predator gate (pre-render packs Predator's 13 000 casters). Report: [`reports/E-94-gpu-pack-wasm.md`](./reports/E-94-gpu-pack-wasm.md).

**Predator gate, E-86 + E-87 + E-91 + E-92 + E-93 (2026-10-23):** **passed (NEUTRAL).** `runPredatorAbab --vs 209df2af --owner preRender`, 4 pairs: logic −4.0 %, spatial −3.6 %, physics −9.3 % (cv 13 %), renderer −5.8 %, pre-render **−1.2 %** (owner; under 3 %, so E-86's 20 000 decorations are no product claim in Predator), each worse in 1 of 4 pairs, load equal. Workers the batch cannot reach moved the same way, so part of it is drift: no regression, no Predator win claimed.

**setSprite's cache key, E-93 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `setSprite` looked its animation index up under `` `${sheetName}:${spriteName}` ``, a new string per call (and not an internalised one, so each lookup hashed it), although the sheet is always `bigAtlas` there. The cache is now keyed by the sprite name alone, in a null-prototype object (a sprite named `constructor` stays an ordinary key). Chrome heap pass, `spawnStormScene` logic **10.6 → 8.5 MB/s** (`setSprite` 2.1 MB/s gone; 24.0 before E-87). Pair (4 pairs): logic **5.328 → 5.140 ms, −3.1 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/1 %, `ENTITIES_PROCESSED` equal (visible entities −4.8 %, a pre-render key).

**Spawn without a closure, E-92 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene), a tie.** `_spawnAt` wrapped its setup in `withBodyDirtyDeferred(() => { … })`, a closure and context per spawn that TurboFan could not inline away (`_spawnAt` is far over the limit). The window is now `beginBodyDirtyDeferred()` / `endBodyDirtyDeferred()` around a `try`/`finally` (the body has no `return`, `this` or `arguments`); `withBodyDirtyDeferred` stays for other callers. Chrome heap pass, `spawnStormScene` logic **17.9 → 10.6 MB/s** (`_spawnAt` 8.6 MB/s gone). Pair (4 pairs): logic **5.243 → 5.234 ms, −0.7 %, worse 1/4**, load within 3 %: a tie, kept as hygiene. Left in that profile: `markBodyDirty`'s `Atomics.or` 3.6 MB/s (E-87), `setSprite`'s string cache key 2.1 MB/s (E-93), `sort` from `_processSpawnSoA` 0.85 MB/s.

**Physics body creation without option objects, E-91 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene).** Once the heap pass sampled the Box2D worker (`a30101ae`), `spawnStormScene`'s physics JS thread showed `createBodyForEntity` as nearly all its allocation: per body an options object of 25 fields, a `{ ...opts, radius }` copy, `createCircle`'s own `{ ...DEFAULT_MATERIAL, ...options }` copy and a discarded `BodyHandle`. It now calls `create_body` / `create_body_box` / `create_body_circle` with the numbers (`createPhysicsApi` returns them as `bodyCreators`); polygons keep the options path. New `tests/node/createBodyDirect.test.js` runs both paths against a recording Module: the same arguments for 256 random bodies (a swapped pair fails it). Chrome heap pass at `?storm=5000` (`7b118a1f`): physics **24.08 → 0.41 MB/s**. Pair (4 pairs, physics owner): **FAIL (load)**: physics 3.843 → 3.912 ms (−0.5 % paired) while B kept **25 % more bodies alive** in every pair (543 → 679; visible entities +7 %): the creates are the same, so B's physics keeps up with more of the one-tick entities. Load-normalised that is −19 % per body, a fit and not a verdict; no worker is worse, so it is kept as hygiene. A load-matched kernel (the extracted `createBodyForEntity` on the real wasm) would decide the speed.

**Free-list heads in the Smi range, E-87 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene), a tie.** Chrome runs `Atomics` as builtins (Node's TurboFan inlines them, so Node never showed it), and a result or argument outside the 31-bit Smi range is a heap number per call. A scratch driver in a Chrome worker: 20 M load + compareExchange + or steps allocated 1.83 GB with values ≥ 2^30 and 0 with small ones, **70 vs 28.5 ns per step**. The u16 free-list head packed a 16-bit tag above the index, so about half the time it was out of range. The tag is now 15 bits (16–30) with bit 31 a copy of bit 30, so the head is always a Smi (tag window 65 536 → 32 768 operations; FL4's 13-bit tag is unchanged, its window is already small). New test: the head stays a Smi through four full tag wraps. Chrome heap pass: `bulletStressScene` particle **2.1 → 0.04 MB/s**, `spawnStormScene` logic 24.0 → 17.9 MB/s (`compareExchange` gone; `Atomics.or` in `markBodyDirty` is next). `spawnStormScene` pair (4 pairs): logic **5.277 → 5.322 ms, +0.7 %**, worse 3/4, with `BODY_COUNT` +3.9 % on B (emergent, within the load tolerance): a tie, kept as hygiene.

**Pre-render sort keys through a typed slot, E-86 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `collectRenderable(type, index, y)` and `_writeRenderable` are far over TurboFan's inline limit, so the double sort key passed per visible particle, LiquidFun particle, bullet (twice), decoration and glow was a heap number per call (the E-85 pattern; sprites pass `0`, a Smi). Callers now write the key into a module `Float64Array` that `_writeRenderable` reads; sprites need none (their drawn Y overwrites it). Same doubles stored, so the queue is unchanged. Chrome heap pass, pre-render: `bulletStressScene` **22.3 → 0.05 MB/s** (first seen once the sampler was fixed, E-88), `liquidFunStressScene` 8.66 → 0.04 MB/s. `bulletStressScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off): pre-render **3.448 → 3.245 ms, −4.0 %, worse 0/4**, cv 3/3 %, `RENDER_QUEUE_SIZE` 32 953 → 32 973. Predator (20 000 decorations through the same call) gates with the next batch.

**Predator gate, W24 + E-85 (2026-10-23):** **passed (NEUTRAL).** `runPredatorAbab --vs 1c610443` (W24, both E-85 commits and their tools), 4 pairs, then 4 more with `--append` because physics read +3.1 % at 2/4 worse: on 8 pairs logic −1.9 %, spatial +0.8 %, renderer +2.4 %, pre-render −1.9 %, physics **+6.7 % at 5/8 worse**, cv 8/6 %, load equal; not a regression by the rule (consistency under 75 %). Physics is reachable only by W24, so W24 sat alone in Predator (A = the pre-W24 Box2D glue and module swapped in, 8 pairs said up front): physics **+2.3 %, worse 4/8**, pair deltas −10.3 % to +17.4 %, every worker under +3 %: NEUTRAL. The batch's +6.7 % was Predator's physics noise; W24 stays kept (hygiene). The first `--append` was refused because a measurement tool was edited mid-sitting (the stamp hashes the whole working-tree diff); setting the edit aside restored the stamp.

**castAll without per-call objects, E-85 second change (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `castAll` cut its "reusable" arrays to length 0 at the start of every call, so it built new hit and result objects every call (the JSDoc promised reuse), and the hit distances were tagged fields. Its closest hits now live in two typed arrays (entity, distance) with the farthest in a typed slot, an insertion sort (stable, as the Array sort was) orders them, and the borrowed results come from a slot pool kept across calls; a caller's `out` keeps its objects too. New `tests/node/rayCastAll.test.js`: brute-force parity with exact distances, and reuse (the old code fails it). Chrome heap pass, `rayStressScene` logic **47.2 → 4.4 MB/s** (77.3 before E-85). Kernel `castAll` **+126.9 %** (cast flat: −1.0 %, a tie). `rayStressScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off): logic **3.692 → 2.741 ms, −25.8 %, worse 0/4**, cv 0/2 %, `BODY_COUNT` 2 000 both sides. The 4.3 MB/s left is the driver's own calls into the public `Ray` methods, which are too big to inline into game code (E-90).

**Ray path without heap numbers, E-85 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** The bullet and ray path boxed two kinds of double. Arguments of `_traverseGrid`, `_checkCellEntities` and `_shapeRayDistance`, which TurboFan does not inline: seven per ray, one per cell, one per candidate. And the global `Infinity` in branches that never run (`tMaxX`, `tDeltaX`, `invDirX` for an axis-aligned ray): the load has no feedback, so the merged variable was typed as any value and the other branch's double boxed. The traced ray now lives in a module `Float64Array` that those functions read (only integers cross the calls), circles and boxes are tested by Ray's own copies of the shape helpers (parity test: equal over 8 000 rays), rare infinities are `const INF`, and castAll's comparator returns a sign. Chrome heap pass: `bulletStressScene` particle **278.5 → 2.1 MB/s**, `rayStressScene` logic 77.3 → 47.2 MB/s. Kernels (checksums equal, 5/5 rounds): ray cast **+20.9 %**, Weed vs Box2D cast 1.00 → 1.21×, bullet tickCrowded **+41.6 %**. `bulletStressScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off): particle **6.593 → 4.873 ms, −26.6 %, worse 0/4**, cv 2/3 %, load equal. A first try (results in typed arrays only) changed nothing: Chrome's heap pass showed the same 279 MB/s. New tool: `tests/bench/heapSampleNode.mjs`, which showed the same sites in Node. Report: [`reports/E-85-ray-path-boxing.md`](./reports/E-85-ray-path-boxing.md). Predator gate with the next batch (W24 + E-85).

**Box2D clock reads, E-55 / E-76 W24 (2026-10-23):** **kept (hygiene), a tie.** Box2D repo `3b2d4c8`: `b2GetTicks` through `emscripten_get_now` (not WASI's `clock_time_get`, a BigInt per call), the per-stage solver timers off in the Weed build (`B2_WEED_NO_STAGE_TIMERS`; step, collide, solve, sleepIslands and sensors still timed), the scheduler's idle spin reading the clock every 64th spin. `ballsHeavyStressScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off): physics **8.861 → 8.734 ms, −2.1 %, worse 1/4**, cv 6/3 %, `BODY_COUNT` 12 004 both sides. Under 3 %: the clock time on the solver threads was inside their 50 µs idle spin, which still spins (E-34). Kept because it removes work nobody reads; Predator gate with the next batch.

**Adobe Animate assets by id, E-84 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Pre-render looked an asset up per character per frame through its name and a string-keyed `Map` (`getAsset` 9.7 %, `getClipFrameRate` 6.0 % self at zoom 1.4, Q1b). `AdobeAnimRegistry.assetById` indexes them by id. `adobeAnimateScene` pair (Chrome, headed, source, 4 pairs, detailed stats off): pre-render **3.514 → 3.214 ms, −9.6 %, worse 0/4**, cv 6/6 %; `BODY_COUNT` and `VISIBLE_ENTITIES` identical; spatial +1.6 % (2/4, cv 18 %), physics −0.0 %. No Predator gate: Predator loads no Adobe Animate asset.

**WASM W25, the spatial gather in home-cell order, E-81 / E-76 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** `order_batch_by_cell` (spatialGather.wasm) sorts each worker's rebuild batch by home cell before `gather_batch`; per-entity results unchanged (parity test both ways). Kernel (`spatialGatherWasmMicrobench`, 3 workers, 16 500 entities, 5 rounds): every list rebuilt **+21.7 %**, Predator's reuse settings +2.3 % (tie). Boids pair (Chrome, headed, source, 6 pairs): spatial **7.462 → 6.942 ms, −8.4 %, worse 0/6**, cv 3/3 %. Predator: one gate NEUTRAL at cv 12–21 % (a busy browser on the machine), one flagged spatial +6.6 % (5/6) with logic +4.1, physics +5.9, pre-render +3.2, renderer +2.1 % (unreachable, same drift); the sort costs 11.4 µs a frame at Predator's batch (~1 200), 0.25 % of spatial. Recorded as drift.

**WASM W13, the particle list build in C, E-80 / E-76 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Stress pair (Chrome, headed, source, `particleIntegrateStressScene?tween=1&spin=1`, 6 pairs, `--vs` the commit before the wiring): particle **4.885 → 4.150 ms, −15.1 %, worse 0/6**, cv 8/2 %; pre-render −2.6 % (0/6); `ACTIVE_PARTICLES` 64 976 → 64 802. Predator gate NEUTRAL: particle under the floor (about 70 live); pre-render +6.1 % (4/6, cv 6/8 %) and physics +3.3 % (3/6), both short of the 75 % of pairs, physics unreachable and pre-render reaching only the ~70-entry visible list; logic −2.7 %, renderer −1.8 %, spatial +2.0 %; load within 0.4 %. Wired as `buildParticleListsWasm` behind `wasm.particleLists` (default on), both published lists in the particle memory; `tests/node/particleListsWasm.test.js` (real workers, three paths, six cameras) is identical frame by frame. Report `reports/E-80-particle-lists-wasm.md`. The kernel sitting: `wasm/particleLists.c`: `buildActiveAndVisibleListBuffers` and `…Turned` over the whole pool, the active column read 16 slots at a time (empty blocks skipped), the screen test in JS's doubles; scalar and f64x2 variants, both bit-identical (both lists and every `isItOnScreen` byte). `particleListsWasmMicrobench`: the `?tween=1` stress population aged 300 frames by the shipped integrate (65 174 live). Node 22, AC, `runKernelVariants`, 5 rounds, JS → scalar → f64x2: stress camera 1 305–1 806 → 5 947–6 061 → 7 419–7 566 ops/s (**+328.6 %**, 25/25); zoomed 763–877 → 5 784–6 045 → 7 180–7 506 (**+764.3 %**; the JS loop branches on the test); turned 3 047–3 881 → 4 390–4 560 → 5 760–5 895 (**+60.4 %**); sparse (Predator's 10 000 slots, 148 live) 57 546–64 756 → 627 599–640 042 → 562 225–756 723 (**+1 070 %**). f64x2 over scalar 25/25 in the dense cases. Chrome decides in the stress-scene pair after the wiring.

**WASM W12, the particle integrate in C, E-79 / E-76 (2026-10-23):** **kept (product).** Stress pair (Chrome, headed, source, `particleIntegrateStressScene?tween=1`, 6 pairs, `--vs` the commit before the wiring): particle **4.249 → 2.935 ms, −31.3 %, worse 0/6**, cv 3/3 %; pre-render +1.3 % (4/6); `ACTIVE_PARTICLES` 64 996 → 64 992. Predator gate NEUTRAL: particle under the floor (about 100 live), logic +2.6 % (5/6), physics +2.2 %, spatial +1.4 %, renderer +0.7 %, pre-render +0.3 %, none at 3 %; load within 0.3 %. Wired as `integrateParticlesWasm` behind `wasm.particleIntegrate` (default on), ParticleComponent in `createParticleMemory`'s `WebAssembly.Memory`; `tests/node/particleIntegrateWasm.test.js` (real worker, both paths) is identical frame by frame. Report `reports/E-79-particle-integrate-wasm.md`. The kernel sitting: `wasm/particleIntegrate.c` is `updateParticlePhysicsBuffers` in double with typed-array store semantics. The dead list is drained through `returnToPool` in JS's order, rare huge angles go back to `Math.*`, and rotation uses inlined fdlibm (`wasm/fdlibmSincos.h`, no stack). `particleIntegrateWasmMicrobench`: the `?tween=1` stress scene's emits through the shipped `ParticleEmitter.emit`, 65 535 particles, `ParticleComponent` in a shared `WebAssembly.Memory`. The particle state is identical after every one of 60 frames. Node 22, AC, `runKernelVariants`, 5 rounds: the stress mix 322.9–367.3 against 510.1–544.9 ops/s, **+61.0 %, 25/25**; with rotation, spin and tint tweens 113.5–127.4 against 241.9–259.4, **+107.9 %, 25/25**. Chrome decides in the stress-scene pair after the wiring.

**WASM W5, the spatial candidate gather in C, E-78 / E-76 (2026-10-22):** **kept (product).** Predator gate (Chrome, headed, source, 6 pairs, owner spatial, `--vs` the commit before the wiring): spatial **5.203 → 4.205 ms, −18.6 % paired, worse 0/6**, cv 3/3 %; physics −3.4 %, renderer −0.3 %, pre-render −1.3 %, logic +4.3 % (4/6, under the 75 % bar; logic reads identical rows, load within 0.05 %); load keys within 0.3 %. The first verdict, FAIL (load), came from a 245 % cv on `FLOWFIELDS_COMPUTED`: median 0 on both sides, one flow field in one baseline run. The nav keys got a gate floor (`452f53c6`) and the recorded pairs re-decided. Wired as `SpatialWorker._findNeighborsWasmGather` behind `wasm.spatialGather` (default on), with the grid in the spatial memory; `tests/node/spatialGatherWasm.test.js` checks both paths frame by frame. Report `reports/E-78-spatial-gather-wasm.md`. The kernel sitting: `wasm/spatialGather.c` does the cell walk, dedup and range test (in double, the JS arithmetic; the worker's circle patterns in their order) for a batch of the entities whose lists rebuild this frame. `spatialGatherWasmMicrobench` runs it as a bench variant on real workers in WASM mode, in a memory laid out as the product would: the publish regions, the grid cells (written there by `rebuildOwnedRows` in both variants), and per worker a marker, truncation flags and a rebuild list, plus the patterns. Rows and counters are equal on every one of 45 frames. Node 22, AC, `runKernelVariants` (alternating processes, 5 rounds), 3 workers, 16 500 entities: Predator's spatial settings with drift, base 267.0–269.4 and WASM 338.2–342.8 frames/s, **+28.0 %, 25/25**; every list rebuilt, +25.5 %, 25/25. Q3's floor said 1.03 for this loop; the WASM gain is not in the arithmetic. Chrome confirms or not in the Predator gate after the wiring.

**Spatial dedup marker as Int32Array, REVIEW_PLAN N-63 (2026-10-22):** fixed, correctness, speed neutral. From stamp frame 4096 the int32 stamp's top bit is set, and the `Uint32Array` marker never matched it, so for 68 s of every 136 s at 60 fps candidates in several cells were appended and published once per cell (Predator mix: 231 453 duplicates in 13 736 of 16 500 rows). Benches never ran long enough to see it. `processedMarker` is now an `Int32Array`. Spatial frame kernel (`spatialWorkerMicrobench` setup, 3 workers, frames below 4096), alternating processes, 4 per side: 252.6–256.2 against 247.4–254.5 frames/s, identical checksums.

**WASM Q3, memory floors, and a calibration of the floor method, E-76 (2026-10-22):** measured (Node 22, AC), premise. `tests/bench/wasmFloorPremise.mjs`. Each shipped loop is set against a patched copy that keeps its loads and stores and drops its float maths (E-18's method). W22, `_updateDecorationSwayBuckets` on a real worker with 20 000 decorations: **1.74–1.77×** its floor's time in snapshot order, 2.51× shuffled. W23, `stampLightRange` on the Predator fixture: the grid walk is only 6.6–6.9 % of the call (selection, nearest tracking and the append are the rest), and 2.20× / 1.48× its two floors. W5, `findNeighborsForOwnedEntities` on a real worker, 16 500 entities, every list rebuilt, the floor replaying the logged decisions so the rows are identical: **1.026–1.035×**. Calibration on `_publishFilteredNeighbors`, the one loop with a shipped WASM twin: floor 1.015–1.033×, while the module runs the same rows **2.10–2.17×** faster (1.43× in `spatialPublishWasmMicrobench`). A floor ratio near 1 therefore does not say WASM cannot win a gather loop. JS's cost on these loops sits around the loads, not in the arithmetic, and the plan's floor screen is withdrawn for them. W5 goes first in Phase 3 (about 18 % of Predator spatial at the publish's 1.4×), W22 stays open, W23 stays borderline, and W10 is screened on its 2.0 % ceiling.

**WASM Q4, the cost of one JS → WASM call, E-76 (2026-10-22):** measured, premise. `tests/bench/wasmCallCostMicrobench.mjs` with the test module `wasm/test/callCost.c`: near-empty exports by argument shape, alternating in one process with their own loop literals (a shared literal made one call site polymorphic, 13–16 ns), against an inlined JS function doing the same adds. Median ns per call, Node 22 / Chrome 155: JS 1.7 / 1.42, `nop0` 0.47 / 0.41 (elided), `sum4i` 3.1 / 3.9, `sum8i` 3.6 / 4.8, `sum4d` 3.4 / 4.4, `sum8d` 4.8 / 5.3, `load1` 3.3 / 4.2. About 4 ns a call: free per frame, batch or light; about 66 µs a frame per entity at 16.5 k, and per particle at 65 k about 6 % of the particle stress worker. W17's per-command export call is not where its cost is.

**WASM Q2, fdlibm trig in WASM against Math.*, E-76 (2026-10-22):** measured, premise. `tests/bench/trigPremise/` builds musl's FreeBSD-derived `atan`, `sin`, `cos` and `asin` into one freestanding module (9 917 bytes), with `atan2` written as V8's ieee754 has it (musl's 2⁶⁴ shortcut cost 1 ulp for 2⁶⁰ < |y/x| < 2⁶⁴). It compares bit for bit on about 10⁸ inputs per function: random bit patterns, the game range ±8π, ±10⁶, [−1, 1], edges near multiples of π/4, and float32 differences for `atan2`. **Node 22 (V8 12.4):** `atan2` 0 of 80.5 M, `atan` 0 of 102 M, `asin` 0 of 102 M; `sin` and `cos` differ by 1 ulp in 0.9–1.5 % of inputs, so V8's are not fdlibm. **Chrome 155 (V8 15.5, Windows):** no function matches. `atan2` differs by 1 ulp in 16.5 % of float32-difference inputs (W1's case) and returns +0 where fdlibm gives −0 for an underflowing negative ratio; `atan` 3.8 % (game range), `asin` 6.0 % ([−1, 1]), `sin`/`cos` about 3 %. After float32 rounding, `sin`/`cos` agree on all 102 M inputs each. WASM float arithmetic is deterministic, so the variable side is Chrome's `Math.*` (other platforms not checked). Rerun: `node tests/bench/trigPremise/node.mjs`; `node tests/bench/chromeKernel.mjs --module /tests/bench/trigPremise/chrome.mjs --args '{"scale":1}'` (about 50 s).

**WASM W8a, the spatial publish in double, E-76 (2026-10-22):** kept (correctness). `wasm/spatialPublish.c` does the JS path's arithmetic (float32 inputs widened, every operation in double, scalar), so the WASM and the JS path publish the same neighbour set; in float32 they differed within ~3·10⁻⁷ of the range edge (Q5). Module 571 bytes (876 before). Kernel, Node 22, AC, against the shipped JS (`spatialPublishWasmMicrobench`): Predator rows +43.1, +45.4, +46.3 % (the float32 module read +41.1 to +45.1 % the same evening); uniform +56.7 to +60.0 % (float32 +85.0 to +87.3 %: the predicate dominates that synthetic case). The same Predator rows in Chrome 155 (`chromeKernel.mjs`, 3 runs): double +2.0, +2.0, +3.2 % faster than float32 (72, 60, 78 of 81). Predator gate (`runPredatorAbab --name W8a`, 6 pairs, owner spatial, headed, source): NEUTRAL. Spatial 5.084 → 5.227 ms, +5.0 % paired but worse in only 4 of 6 pairs, under the 75 % consistency bar. Logic −3.8 %, renderer −2.0 %, physics +0.2 %, pre-render +0.4 %; `BODY_COUNT`, `VISIBLE_ENTITIES`, `GPU_CASTERS` all within 0.0 %. The kernel is no slower in Chrome on the scene's own distribution, and the published rows are the same, so nothing downstream changed. Read the spatial +5.0 % as the gate's noise; if a later Predator sitting that touches spatial shows it again, measure this again.

**WASM Q5, the float32 publish against the double JS path, E-76 (2026-10-22):** measured (Node), correctness. The shipped module computes `dx² + dy² < er²` in float32. The JS path (`_publishFilteredNeighbors`, taken at 32-bit ids or with `wasm.spatialPublish: false`) computes it in double from the same float32 inputs. Both predicates were evaluated exactly for every candidate the real gather produced over 45 frames of the Predator mix (`spatialWorkerMicrobench` setup, 3 workers): 34 918 287 pairs, **0 disagreements**. Of 3 000 000 pairs placed one float32 step from the edge, 26 957 disagree (0.9 %; 22 497 inside only in double, 4 460 only in float32), all within 3.1·10⁻⁷ of the edge relative to er², about 2.6 float32 steps. On a 2 000-entity edge frame the shipped rows differ from the JS rows for 86 entities. Two double kernels (scratch builds with the pinned flags; the JS arithmetic in C, float32 inputs widened, every operation in double) publish rows identical to the JS path on both frames. Cost, Node 22 (V8 12.4), TurboFan checked, AC power, 3 processes of 9 alternating rounds. On the Predator rows, scalar double read **−1.9, +0.5 and +0.8 %** against the shipped f32x4 module (tie), `f64x2` −3.6 to −5.1 %, the JS path −28.8 to −31.0 %. On the edge frame (sequential candidates, half of them inside, so the arithmetic dominates), scalar double read −21.6 %, `f64x2` −22.3 % and JS −44.7 %. The paths disagree only at the edge and never in a real frame on record. The double kernel costs nothing measurable where the gather's loads dominate, which is the product's case. Next: W8a, the C in double as a correctness change, through the Predator gate.

**WASM spatial publish kernel against the shipped JS, E-76 T4 (2026-10-22):** measured (kernel), no change. Until now `spatialPublishWasmMicrobench` timed the WASM against a JS copy of the publish loop. On the T4 harness it times `SpatialWorker._publishFilteredNeighbors` on a real worker in JS mode (the path taken without a publish memory) against `publish_batch` on the product memory. Rows are compared before timing, and the sides alternate in one process for 9 rounds. Node 22 (V8 12.4), WASM checked to be TurboFan (`--allow-natives-syntax`), laptop **on battery**. Uniform case (the 2026-10-06 data: 4 096 entities, 48 random candidates each, batch 2 048), three processes: the shipped JS ran at 2 315–3 068 ops/s and the WASM at 4 015–5 611, **+73.5 %, +82.9 %, +87.4 %**, each 81/81 cross comparisons. The old bench, alternated with it, read +69 %, +123 % and +138 %, its JS copy at about 2 000 ops/s (the copy builds five typed-array views per call). Predator case (the candidate rows the real gather builds for one worker's 4 677 owned entities, 264 851 neighbours published): JS 379, WASM 630 ops/s, **+66.4 %** (81/81). So the +118 % on record was measured against the copy; against the shipped JS the kernel edge is smaller. The scene keep of 2026-10-06 (Predator spatial −14.6 %) stands on its own pair. **On AC power, the same evening**, three processes per case: uniform JS 4 065–4 087 and WASM 7 533–7 628 ops/s, **+85.0, +86.6, +87.3 %**; Predator JS 670–704 and WASM 959–993, **+41.1, +45.1, +43.2 %**, all 81/81. Battery had halved the clock and inflated the Predator edge (+66.4 % from one process).

**WASM migration Phase 0 and Q1, E-76 (2026-10-22):** measured (profiles), no change. Every share on record predated E-77, so four headed CPU profiles ran on `5477a582` (source, detailed stats off, 10 s warm-up, 10 s window, about 45 s each; `tests/results/wasm-q1/`). The profiler inflates `STEP_MS`, so only shares were read. Predator: spatial `findNeighborsForOwnedEntities` 61.8 % self (85.8 % inclusive), the WASM publish 17.5 %, `rebuildOwnedRows` 12.5 %; pre-render `emitSpriteQueue` 28.7 % inclusive, `collectVisibleEntities` 32.1 % inclusive (7.2 % self), shadow-caster stamp 25.9 % inclusive; Pixi `packInstancedRows` 33.9 %, painter order 11.8 %; particle `_updateDecorationSwayBuckets` 45.1 %, particle integrate 0.7 %; logic contacts 11.3 %; `publishPose` ~8 % of the physics JS thread. VisPolyStressScene pre-render: the per-light sweep 49.2 %, the neighbour gather 26 % plus `_displayPose` 13.7 %. CompoundVisPoly `ringSegments=64` pre-render: `collectSolidSegments` 84.9 % inclusive, and one line of it, the per-edge `normalizeAngleSigned(Math.atan2(…))`, about 38 % of the worker (V8 runs `Math.atan2` as its native fdlibm port, which WASM can at best match). ParticleIntegrate `tween=1` particle: integrate 75.3 %, list build 22.6 %. What it changed in the plan: W12/W13 lead Phase 3 (ceiling ~17 % + ~7 % in the stress scene), then W2a (~11 %) and W1 (~10 % after the `atan2` is set aside); two candidates the plan had not listed, decoration sway (W22) and the shadow-caster stamp (W23); five parks below. Plan and per-candidate facts: `WASM_HYPOTHESES.md`, "Phase 0 inventory".

**WASM W3, the cull loop of `collectVisibleEntities` (2026-10-22):** screened, ceiling. Q1 Predator: the loop is 7.2 % self of pre-render; at the 1.2× that a ~9-column gather with trivial arithmetic could plausibly gain in plain WASM, that is 1.2 % of the worker. The rest of its 32.1 % inclusive is `collectRenderable` → `_writeRenderable`, which reads JS objects per entity and stays JS. Reopen if a cull-dominated scene shows the loop's self time at 18 % or more of pre-render, and then run Q3 first. The whole render-queue build (collect plus emit, 61 % of Predator pre-render) stays open as W4, blocked on the memory decision.

**WASM W9, `packInstancedRows` in Pixi (2026-10-22):** screened, ceiling. Q1 Predator: 33.9 % of Pixi. E-18 measured that arithmetic is 26 % of the call (painter pack 0.497 ms, gather-only 0.367, identity 0.287) and the rest is the gather through the painter list; X5 showed that cost is cache lines pre-render wrote on another core. Plain WASM at 1.2× on the arithmetic saves 33.9 % × 26 % × 0.17 ≈ 1.5 % of Pixi; 3 % needs that arithmetic 1.42× faster than TurboFan, which nothing on record supports. Reopen if a WASM vertex upload path makes the copy in `_commitPackedBatch` go away (a different claim).

**WASM W14, navigation search (2026-10-22):** screened, ceiling. Q1 Predator: navigation is 5.9 % inclusive of the particle worker (`computeFlowfield` 3.7 %), so 1.0 % at 1.2×. In NavStress the search runs against an 8 ms deadline (E-38), so a faster search computes more flow fields per frame and `STEP_MS` cannot fall. Reopen if flow-field throughput (`FLOWFIELDS_COMPUTED`) becomes the question. The search lives in `particleWorker.js` (`computeFlowfield`, `computePath`), not `navGrid.js`.

**WASM W15, `publishPose` in C (2026-10-22):** screened, ceiling. Q1 Predator: `publishPose` took 558 ms of the physics JS thread in the window, about 8 % of it; at 1.3× that is 1.8 %. PH1 (2026-10-01) already showed the limit: kernel +147.1 %, Predator physics −0.8 % (4/8 worse), because Predator respawns all the time and the epoch fast path never latches. A C copy also cannot write the pose SABs from Box2D's memory. Reopen only if the memory decision (E-76 Phase 4) puts the pose buffers in Box2D's heap, and then as a copy removal, not a language claim.

**WASM W20, audio mixer (2026-10-22):** screened, parked. The worklet is in no `STEP_MS` and absent from every trace; 128 voices take about 0.1 ms of a 2.67 ms quantum (`audioMixerMicrobench` 9 527 ops/s, which times `_mixDirect`, not the product's `_mixBuses`). The claim was predictability (no GC or tier-up stalls), not speed. Reopen if glitches are heard.

**WASM W21b, W21d, W21e: triangulation, Adobe compile, atlas bake (2026-10-22):** screened, boot only. None runs per frame: mesh triangulation runs when game code calls `MeshRenderer.setPolygon`, `AdobeAnimCompiler.compile` once per Adobe asset at preload, the atlas bake only when no baked atlas exists (Predator and littleCity ship baked atlases). The plan judges load work on boot time, not `STEP_MS`. Reopen when someone reports a slow boot, and measure boot time.

**Imports read as module consts, E-77 (2026-10-22):** kept. `spatialWorker`, `preRenderWorker`, `particleWorker` and `colliderUtils` re-declare the imports their hot methods read (`import { Grid as GridImport } …; const Grid = GridImport;`). Why the bundle beat source: a native import is a cell TurboFan reads at run time, with a TDZ check; webpack's getter is inlined and folded, and so is the class's typed array behind it (`rebuildOwnedRows` 5 564 B of TurboFan code in the bundle against 6 940 B in source, `collectVisibleEntities` 6 496 against 8 256). A module const folds in both forms. Source, MinimalTick, 3 rounds: spatial −16.2 % (3/3), pre-render −22.7 % (3/3); Predator headed, 6 rounds: particle 1.380 ms −27.6 % (6/6), pre-render −6.1 % (4/6), spatial −3.5 % (4/6), logic per 1000 entities +3.4 / +1.0 % (2/6, loads 2482–2950, no import changed in logic). Bundle, Predator headed, 6 rounds: particle 1.289 ms −17.9 % (6/6): its large integrate functions inlined nothing when their helpers came through getters and inline `returnToPool`'s chain now; the other workers within noise (pre-render +6.0 %, 1/6, while its hot functions compile to the same 6 496 B and MinimalTick bundle pre-render was −5.2 %). Bundle +4.2 KB (2 966 778 → 2 971 012). Copying an import into a local at function entry instead: spatial −2 to −6 %, not reproduced. All imports in Pixi, logic and `abstractWorker` as well: nothing more measurable there (their imports are read per frame). Self-contained workers (no `worker_common` chunk, full scope hoisting): bundle +64 %, rejected for size. Not visible in Node (V8 12.4): the spatial kernel tied across all four forms. Guard: `tests/node/importAliases.test.js` (no alias inside the 19-module import cycle, none of an `export let`). Rules in HOW_WE_MEASURE, "Imports in hot code".

**seededRandom state wraps to 32 bits (2026-10-22):** fixed, correctness, speed neutral. The mulberry32 state was a closure double that never wrapped; it passed 2^53 at call 4 917 760 of a stream, and from there the sequence was no longer mulberry32 (and would in the end stop changing). Now an `Int32Array(1)` that wraps like the reference; the first 4 917 760 outputs of every stream are unchanged. MinimalTick logic (one `rng()` per entity per tick): source +2.5 % (0/3), bundle −1.3 % (2/3).

**Source modules against the bundle (2026-10-22):** measured, no change (explained the same day by E-77 above). The same tree, headless, `--src` against `dist/` rebuilt from it, 4 pairs alternating, 6 s warm-up, 8 s window, detailed stats off. Predator, bundle against source per worker (`STEP_MS − WAIT_MS`): preRender 7.42 ms −12.0 % (0/4 slower), physics 7.81 −6.5 % (1/4), spatial 6.32 −3.8 % (1/4), renderer 4.51 −3.3 % (1/4), logic1 4.08 −5.9 % (1/4, load +4.5 %); logic0 4.44 −15.9 % does not count (its `ENTITIES_PROCESSED` moved −8.3 %). bunnyMark (screening, logic under the 3 ms floor): logic0 2.78 ms +3.9 % (3/4 slower), preRender +16.8 % (2/4, noise), renderer −7.2 % (0/4). Every scene row in this log is source mode; games from npm run the bundle, which is not slower. The bundle writes `const { … } = WEED` as `var` (captured, mutable) where source mode has module consts; bunnyMark's logic hint points there and needs a heavier logic load to judge. Running it found that Predator and six other demos could not load from the bundle at all (N-57).

**setAnimation without a string per call, N-58 (2026-10-22):** kept (working tree). Kernel `setAnimationMicrobench.mjs` (4096 entities, 8 animations, real GameObject and registry, normal compile, checksum 1945521605 both sides): same animation every call 7.5–8.0 M → 48–57 M calls/s, minor GCs in the samples 7 → 0; a new animation every call 3.4–3.6 M → 7.1–7.4 M.
